Point Report of the Transport Committee on the Government Template (2110 e.g._Other Organiser The Federal Act establishing a federal law on rail transport and passenger rights and amending the Railway Act 1957, and the Amendment 1786/A(E) by Mrs Dr Gabriela Moser, colleagues on the further development of passenger rights (2118 d.b.)
ParlaMint-AT_2013-01-31-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00188_d7e8873	We're now on the 1st. Agenda item. There was no oral reporting. We are going into the debate. First of all, Mr Heinzl. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2013-01-31-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00188_d7e8903	Madam President. Thank you, Mrs. Federal Minister! High house! Ladies and gentlemen! Since 2010, long-distance rail customers have received 25 percent of the price of travel at a delay of one hour and even 50 percent from two hours. Also since 2010 owners of annual tickets in local transport receive a compensation of 10 percent, if the trains on their route are not at least 90 percent on time. [[President Neugebauer shall take over the chairmanship.]] This law, ladies and gentlemen, was really a great success. As early as 2011, the ÖBB were as timely as ever. 97 percent of all trains arrive at their destination on time, and the same applies to 2012. Thus, it can be noted with joy that the ÖBB are the most punctual railway in Europe. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ.]] In the few cases, ladies and gentlemen, where delays occurred, a compensation of € 365 000 was paid in the area of long-distance transport, and in regional transport, 1 300 commuters with a total of €29 200 were compensated. Ladies and gentlemen! High house! With the current novel, the rights of the commuters will be strengthened again. About 540 000 people benefit from this every day. Reliability on the way to work is particularly important for the commuters. In the future, compensation is already paid if punctuality falls below 95 percent instead of 90 percent. Also new, ladies and gentlemen: Pendlers with annual cards will be able to check at any time on the Internet free of charge whether the specified punctuality level is actually achieved on their route. The annual ticket owners must register only once in the system, then they automatically receive their compensation at the end of the year. Important, ladies and gentlemen: The new rules apply to all railway undertakings and not only to the ÖBB. The passenger board is also new. This advisory board not only controls the punctuality, but also the cleanliness in the trains, the technical condition and the information for the passengers. Ladies and gentlemen! High house! The novelties to be decided today give the 540 000 commuters even more rights, and I believe that this is another step to establish the railway as an environmentally friendly, modern and, above all, customer-friendly transport.

Therefore, the bike package, as I believe, is the right and modern answer to create more safety and clear rules for cyclists. Point Report by the Justice Committee on the Citizens' Initiative No. 37/BI: ‘Stops storage of stocks’ (2086, e.g.)
ParlaMint-AT_2013-01-31-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00188_d7e13518	We're now on the 9th. Agenda item. There was no oral reporting. Mr Magistrate has spoken on behalf of the Group of the European People's Party (Christian Democrats). Steinhauser. 6 minutes speaking time. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2013-01-31-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00188_d7e13545	Ladies and gentlemen! Last year 106 067 citizens signed the citizens’ initiative “Stop the storage of data” which is the largest citizens’ initiative ever addressed to Parliament. And it is not a coincidence that this citizens' initiative has received so much support, because the storage of data as a monitoring measure is now facing massive resistance. What is the data storage of stocks? – The storage of stock data is nothing but the suspiciously independent storage of mobile and Internet data as well as location data, which allows you to determine who was where and who wrote when or who telephoned when – without the persons concerned being blamed. The only thing you can blame them is that they use mobile phones or the Internet. The citizens' initiative has had two clear objectives: One goal was to work in Brussels to amend the data storage directive, because Austrian legislation is based on a directive of the European Union. The second concern was to evaluate the law on terror, i.e. the law on monitoring in Austria. There was then quite a hearing with 16 experts in the judicial committee. The critics of data storage have found clear words. I just want to quote a few things. That's what it said: The rule of law of terrorists is small. Or worded by experts: That's the police state in the drawer, that's Stasi 2.0. Or: Criminals know what measures are being taken to deal with data storage of stocks, and non-proliferation of the public is taken. These were the opinions of independent experts who were critical. There have also been persons in the hearing who were for storage of stock data. The notable thing, however, was that all these were officials from various ministries and the legal guards. So that the legal guardians are euphoric about the storage of data, that makes me in principle skeptical, but whether it is. But the only advocates were the representatives of the ministry. The representative of the Ministry of Infrastructure has said that he is already glad that the constitutional implementation of the data storage is argumentable. So the claim is obviously very low. The representative of the Ministry of Justice was a great supporter of data storage. He spoke with shining eyes of the benefits. And he was so euphoric that he said, in the Ministry of Justice, one can even imagine using stock data in the future even in copyright infringements. That's exactly what we've always criticized. We have said that the Pandora can be opened, that they start with criminal law and then end up in the enforcement of civil rights claims and a massive fundamental legal intervention, a massive expansion of the surveillance state. This tension ratio must be constantly carefully balanced. I remember that data storage is actually a result of those terrorist attacks by London and Madrid. With reference to the Convention on Human Rights and the Charter of Fundamental Rights of the European Union, this should be easy. There's nothing we have to wait for, because we don't have to wait for anything, but I hope that the Constitutional Court will still come to our side here. The second point – that I do not forget – is the evaluation of all those measures that are intervention into fundamental and freedom rights. We have already introduced this request here in Parliament quite a year ago, because we believe that all these measures – terrorism prevention and so on – must be subject to a clear control of fundamental rights, including the fundamental right of freedom of expression and so on. It must be clear that, of course, the interventions in fundamental and freedom rights are made to a minimum. There's the big scale. The security of the population is evident to us liberals, as we always see here, a very big concern. However, it must be achieved by appropriate and appropriate means, as the treatment of fundamental and freedom rights is ultimately also a measure of the maturity of a democratic rule of law. We will therefore continue to see that these interventions are made to the minimum possible extent.

On the issue of the effectiveness of access to data on telecommunications, I would like to point out that it has been so far – that is, over many years before the 1st century. April 2012 – allowed and common practice was that the law enforcement authorities received information in the specific case of suspicion and, of course, only in the event of compliance with the relevant legal requirements concerning the data available from the operator for operational purposes. This investigation has already been an extremely effective means of persecuting and investigating serious crime, such as murder, robbery, burglary and sexual offences. In my view, the implementation of the Stockpiles Data Storage Directive has also led to increased fundamental rights protection and greater legal clarity for providers and authorities in Austria, with particular emphasis being placed on the comprehensive control powers of the legal guardian of justice. The technical conditions for data transmission created to ensure high data security – i.e. the encrypted transmission of the data from the providers to the law enforcement authorities – must also be considered as exemplary. High house! Experience so far in the application of the provisions on information on stocks has shown that law enforcement authorities really deal with this instrument. The Legal Protection Officer of the Justice reports that he is on the 31st of July. ==References====External links==* Official website 2a StPO – including cases of robbery, rape and drug trafficking. It was not just one-bright opinion that they are conducive to the investigation, in particular the investigation of other stakeholders, but rather the need for retrospective investigation measures was in doubt. This is particularly true in the number of cases of heavy theft with large amounts of damage and heavy robbery, partly obviously committed by foreign gangs, with a lack of fear of unparticipation in these cases, because the investigations de facto are limited only to communications between the complications. The protection of the privacy of the unparticipant is ensured by the requirement of judicial authorisation, the examination by the legal protection officer and the audit of the outcome by him. As far as I am concerned so far, there is no doubt that, as far as my area of competence is concerned, we have a comprehensive legal protection against access to stock records for the purposes of law enforcement and that the use of stock records in certain cases clearly defined by the law is indeed a necessary instrument for the effective persecution of serious and severest crime, which has proven itself in practice in recent months. The balance between the individual's rights to respect his rights to privacy and privacy on the one hand and the legitimate interest of the State and the victims concerned in the pursuit and investigation of serious and most serious crimes is actually observed in criminal proceedings under the framework of the directive. If doubts are now expressed by the political groups represented here about the balance between individual rights and the purposes of the fight against crime and prosecution, and if there is a need for change, we are happy to have this discussion, because the protection of human rights is a great asset and, of course, a measure of fair justice. However, it will be awaiting how the European Court of Justice will assess the rules of the Directive with a view to maintaining this balance. However, we must not give up the protection function of criminal law and its enforcement, as victims of serious crime also have a right to the State and its institutions having limited and controlled access to data from telecommunications for the purpose of investigating and persecuting these acts. Thank you.

Put into effect on April 2012, on the basis of the most necessary requirements with the shortest storage time. In essence, the obligation to store for the purpose of identifying, identifying and persecuting serious crimes applies, which in principle means that these data should be used to combat terrorism. We are all aware that an increased risk of terrorist activities in Europe has posed new challenges to the judicial and security authorities over the past 15 years. We need politicians to work best with the judiciary and find solutions that are satisfactory for justice and, of course, for the protection of citizens. Instead of a general storage, for example, an incidental processing would be possible if the suspicion was properly examined. It is therefore necessary to ensure that there are no unauthorised access and that the data are then deleted again. To ensure that these data cannot somehow be misused is difficult because with the possibility of storage, we also create the basic possibility of abuse. At the hearing in the Justice Committee in November, the Legal Protection Officer at the Ministry of Justice informed us that there have been 188 surveys by judicial and security authorities since the introduction of the stock data storage. In 19 cases there has been an investigation until that date. The Minister has already mentioned this; I would like to put it a little more precisely: In three of the 168 cases it was about murder, in 58 about heavy theft, in 14 about heavy robbery, in 20 about stalking, in 16 about serious fraud, in 20 for violations against the drug law and in ten for rape. The cases identified by 19 to that time were stalking by seven. In any case, I do not want to impoverish these crimes, but it is obvious that these cases are not about combating terrorism, and this was the real reason why data storage on the EU's part was required. Now the real problem of data storage is becoming visible, as the EU Charter of Fundamental Rights guarantees – such as the European Convention on Human Rights and the fundamental right to data protection enshrined in the Austrian Constitution – that each person has the right to the protection of the data concerning him. As the above evaluation shows, data storage affects almost exclusively persons who have not given rise to data storage. The authorities identify their data and are informed of the private behaviour of such persons, and then the risk of abuse. I would like to quote the President of the Constitutional Court of Gerhart Holzinger, who was in a press release of 18 November. December 2012 writes the following: ‘The Constitutional Court is obliged to turn the ECJ on if it doubts whether it is valid or not. the interpretation of Union law. We have doubts that the EU's Data Storage Directive is truly compatible with the rights guaranteed by the EU Fundamental Rights Charter." There's nothing to add to that.

ParlaMint-AT_2013-01-31-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00188_d7e19820	Ladies and gentlemen! The important thing about Austria's art policy, which can be read from the art report, is that the good framework conditions for artists and artists will be maintained even for art visitors in economically difficult times. This exemplary art policy can only be understood in comparison with the other European countries. With the exception of France, which itself has a very high artistic and cultural budget and is moving at a very high level, almost all EU countries have made massive cuts in budgets due to national savings plans and savings projects. In Hungary, for example, the promotion of art and culture has been reduced by almost 10 percent from a very low starting level. In Italy, Series level first reduced by 40 percent; this had to be reversed due to the great protests. Spain: more than 12 percent, United Kingdom: 15 percent. This list could still be continued. In view of these comparisons, the very high importance which art and culture also enjoy materially in Austria is shown. Let us now look at the area of “presentative art”! I compared the 2007/2008 art report with the 2010/2011 art report with regard to individual funding. The following can be found: The amount is commuting from € 17.54 million to € 19.13 million in 2010/2011. In 2008, twelve artists were funded individually, and in 2011 it was nine. Among the artists it was 26 and that has risen to 30. The average amount of funding has, of course, increased: In 2008, every artist received €5,610, in 2011, €5,910; this is an increase of 5.3 percent. Among the artists, the increase was much higher: from €5 680 a year to €6 700, i.e. an increase of more than 18 percent. I think this shows that there is very much budgetary, emancipatory policy in this area, and this includes our Minister's recognition, but also the cultural section and the advisory committees and the jury who have provided it. [[Congratulations with SPÖ and ÖVP.]] I am particularly pleased that former State Secretary Wolfgang Waldner, quoted in the “Kurier” yesterday, in the following words – and with this he has taken over a long-standing social democratic commitment – referred to the art: One had to “trap down the imaginary wall between politics and art ” and“ learn to appreciate artists as seismographs of society. ” - Quote. So you see, social-democratic art and cultural policy works. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2013-01-31-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00188_d7e20186	Mr President. Thank you, Mrs. Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! We have heard it, with the Bundesmuseen Act two points are decided: on the one hand, that parts of the former Courtroom Archive of the Austrian National Library are transferred for use, and on the other hand, there is a formal adjustment in connection with the operation of the newly opened 21st House and the Austrian Gallery Belvedere. In a building that has been empty for years, in the former Hofkammerarchiv, the Austrian National Library plans to set up a literature museum. Such a museum is lacking in Austria, a country that has so many important authors, not only one but many. Literature from Austria is a key factor in determining Austrian identity both nationally and internationally. However, while visual art and music have appropriate exhibition, performance or event houses, similar institutions are missing for literature in Austria to present them to the public. What is more obvious than that the Austrian National Library operates an Austrian Literary Museum? It also brings essential prerequisites. The Austrian Literary Archive and the manuscript collection of the Austrian National Library host important literary treasures that can be presented in the museum. Since the Austrian National Library operates three museums, it also brings the appropriate know-how for this ambitious project. The museum is to be set up on three floors of the former Hofkammerarchiv and is intended to convey to the public the complex facts that literature from Austria represents, relevant and sustainable. Several functions will be carried out: Exhibition, mediation and research. In principle, the implementation of a permanent exhibition, accompanied by thematic exchange exhibitions, is planned. Exhibits can be taken over from Germany and abroad. The following will now surely be pleased with my colleague, Mr Unterlander: The existing barbecue room is also integrated into the museum as a representation of the place of work of an important author – you have said it. This would also be the case. This historic space is guaranteed in which Grillparzer – you have said it yourself – served as Director of the Court Chamber Archive from 1832 to 1856. The original equipment, including a stand-up board, on which Grillparzer wrote some of his dramas, is by the way still well preserved and is also presented there. I think that also fits into the Literary Museum. [[Intrusion of the Abg. May. Unspoiled.]] Where else, if not in a literature museum, should this be and be presented and reported about the important poet? Special attention will also be paid to the mediation function. With the help of contemporary media communication such as touchscreens, interactive reading terminals, hearing stations, film displays, audio guides and the like, the literature museum is intended to give all school types insight into the literary field. The museum will also become a meeting place for international scientific symposia. There have so far been no suitable premises for this purpose. It is intended to be a place of research that reaches a broad public through publications on permanent and exchange exhibitions. Finally, I would like to say so much to the 21st house: The 21st house is a place of production, reception and reflection of Austrian art of 20 and 21. ==References====External links== The great cultural importance of the 21st house is also reflected in the investments made by the BMUKK – around €16.5 million have been invested here. The opening of the 21st house was a very large cultural milestone, and this also brings me to another one that is about to come, namely the opening of the Kunstkammer on 28 May. February. In this sense, I can only say: Go and look at the exhibitions, and enjoy!

Point Report of the Committee on the Environment, Public Health and Consumer Protection on the Government template (1993_Other Organiser Federal Law on the placing on the market of gas oils on inland waterways and sports boats, as well as on mobile machinery and equipment (2130 d.b.)
ParlaMint-AT_2013-01-31-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00188_d7e23981	We reach the 17th. Agenda item. There was no oral reporting. We are going into the debate. First of all, Mr Ing. Schools. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2013-01-31-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00188_d7e24011	(Parliament adopted the legislative resolution) Estimated Minister of State! High house! Ladies and gentlemen, we are discussing today a legal provision that will not touch you very much. In the future, the fuel of ships operating on our Danube or on other waters shall not contain more than 10 grams of sulphur per tonne of fuel. You will say: Who cares? I have to say that the oil tankers or container ships that travel on the large oceans have 35 kilograms of sulphur per tonne, and the daily consumption of such an oil tanker is more than 300 tons of oil. That is, he brings 12 tons of sulphur into the environment every day. This is an issue that must not be underestimated, because 5% of world oil stocks are consumed via shipping; and the sulphur in oil is ultimately the disposal potential of many refineries. It will therefore be necessary to advance the implementation in this area, the desulphurisation of these fuels as well. The “Costa Concordia”, which is in the water in Italy, had 2,380 cubic meters of fuel with corresponding sulphur content on board – the more sulphur the cheaper. The ships are actually waste disposal factories on the water. There is something to be done, and I think that the European Union is just the right lever for this. is to carry things on. That's what it would be like. - Thank you.
ParlaMint-AT_2013-01-31-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00188_d7e24051	Next speaker: Mr Wenninger. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2013-01-31-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00188_d7e24072	Madam President. Minister! Thank my colleague, Schultes, for explaining this law in such detail. We are implementing an EU directive and, by this law, we are creating a scheme to reduce the sulphur content in fuels, which is reduced from 1 000 milligrams per kilogram to only 10 milligrams per kilogram in boats, but also in mobile equipment and machinery, for example in construction and agriculture. I believe that this can lead to a significant improvement in air quality in Austria, but also across Europe. The discussion in the Committee was very constructive, and I assume that the proposal will be approved unanimously in the House and thank you very much.

Impacts are very likely to be respiratory and cardiovascular diseases, which in turn reduces the life expectancy of Europeans. The maximum sulphur content of gas oil is expected to be ten milligrams per kilogram in the future – we have heard it several times today. This concerns inland waterways and sports boats which are not at sea, but also the operation of mobile machines and equipment. A key point in this law is also the control of gas oils. Sampling may be carried out at scheduled gas stations and at charging points. This law is intended to contribute to better air quality, and nearly half of all Europeans are still living in areas where the EU's air quality objectives have not yet been achieved. Finally, I would like to stress that it is particularly important for me that, among other things, increasing shipping is also making an appropriate contribution to this law. Thank you.

What's inside the cosmetic bag? We have already heard: People's desires are now being treated better. This is to be welcomed, not a question. But that is, with confidence, almost self-evident, what comes here, namely, that the initiators have a right of speech and that they hold a special session on it. This is to be welcomed, but not a big step. Electoral evidence is now being improved and unified throughout Austria. That is right, but not a big step in the sense of direct democracy. The citizens' initiative is now easier to support, easier to support electronically. The preferred votes will be better evaluated. And then something really new comes, namely the citizens' request. The citizens' request to explain this briefly is a very great thing. Citizen's request means that between 28 and 28 years of age. ==References== Questions may be sent to Parliament by the day before the next meeting. There will be a homepage of its own. The person who submits these requests must have a qualified electronic signature and may be supported in seven days before the meeting. You know, that's a very simple system, easy to see. At least 10 000 citizens, in turn with qualified electronic signatures, must simply support this request. This is probably as similar as Facebook, because they have to press a “like” and if at least 10 000 supporters have supported this request, there is the possibility that it will then come to the call in a kind of Question Time, in a citizen’s request. However, the questions will be put at the meeting after approval. The seven best questions will be arranged. It is not true, however, that the questioner then stands here and asks this question, but it is then read by a Member and the Minister has to answer. Then Members can ask their supplementary questions, and then the minutes will be sent to the person who has put this question, supported by 10,000 or more supporters. This is now the great innovation, the citizen participation. We're all impressed. That's what's really new, and that's the cosmetic bag. We had set up a working group that really – I have to say now – started to work constructively. At first, the ideas for direct democracy were brought in by all the parties. There are very concrete ideas, which were not only created last summer, but are much lower. Of us, for example, there has already been a very concrete proposal for about one year, how direct democracy can be further developed – that is, really further developed, not just reformed, brought back in some form, but developed further. They're already here. There are concrete proposals from the Greens. That means there was already something we could talk about. In this committee we had the impression that there was an open discussion, so we also said from the outset that we can agree on a few easier exercises, but it must always be clearly the big goal: What is really the next decisive step? And the decisive next step is the People's Initiative, which means the possibility that a legislative initiative can really come out of the population, and as a second point the veto vote, that is the possibility of bringing an already adopted law with a certain participation of the population to a referendum. [[Admissibility at the FPÖ]] These are the two essential points, and we have said of the opposition: Yes, we like to discuss here, but it must be clear that the discussion is going on here too and not staying at a very early point. We had certain hopes, because there was the ÖVP short paper, in which this popular initiative was inside, with a very high approval quo, but nevertheless. Madam President Prammer, too, launched a press service in May 2012, which clearly showed that it found the popular initiative positive and desirable. And then a withdrawal was made. It was probably also a bit of this popular survey, which did not go out as it wanted at least a part of it inside. It says again and again that the political elites are so long for direct democracy as their opinion is supported; once it is not, then you get cold feet. It was made the great withdrawal. There was then no joint outcome of this working group, but suddenly there was only one result of two parties. Here it is referred to as the “Government template Brief”, but I think that was just a promise, so a presentation came from the government parties. We were also equal in this working group. So, I am very glad that we are also equal in the committees, but I think it is nice that this has been emphasized. Perhaps this is the way of thinking that otherwise prevails, and perhaps we only imagine that we are otherwise equal. At any rate, there was no joint outcome of this working group, but suddenly there was a result that two government parties presented – that is, this cosmetic bag I just described – and nothing else. Then, of course, the disappointment was huge, because you see, the will to further development is not really there, but you want to cut things off here, you want to show the population: We did something. Please, the government parties are really for direct democracy. We have now really invented this citizens' request, and it will therefore be revolutionaryally involving citizens in political will-building. That's not what we want, it's simple and simple, it's not an essential step at all. That is why I can only say: For real development of direct democracy we are to have, at least not for pure cosmetics.
ParlaMint-AT_2013-01-31-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00188_d7e24886	Mrs Mag. Musiol – Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2013-01-31-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00188_d7e24907	Madam President. Yes, the package presented has already received many names: “Cosmetic bag” and much more. I do not want to go looking for this right now, but a reform is clearly not that; at least no reform, as we Greens imagine, as I imagine it. What are you doing here? – This more than one year public debate on direct democracy is not based on the boredom of individuals, but on a group of people who have spoken and said, once again – not for the first time, but once again – about the last popular desire: It can't be that it's in a modern democratic state in the 21st century. The century still is that people even go to a municipal office, so take on several obstacles here to support a concern that is important to them – we have different views on how to judge this educational people’s desire! –, more as 300,000 people, almost 400,000 people then come here and almost as an exceptional situation a special meeting is held, a special committee is held, the proponents of popular desire have to sit up there and listen to our statements so that nothing comes out in the end. 380 000 people who signed this did not deserve it! And the many others who want to be involved in this issue have not gone at all because they know it doesn't work anyway because we now have 34 people who have been treated the same way. That's where the debate started. [[Congratulation with the greens.]] Then came various proposals from us Greens, from various initiatives, also from State Secretary Kurz, from President Prammer, from FPÖ and how we are all sitting here. And that you, Mr Gerstl, now mention the proposal of the Secretary of State, Mr Kurz here again, is quite courageous, because if you look at what the Young ÖVP has proposed and at that time also was very much supported by representatives of the ÖVP and what you are now delivering, then you can see: There are worlds in between. This is not even a successful general sample and an absolutely unsuccessful idea, which has almost nothing to do with each other at all. What is needed to support the package and the proposals discussed last year, and what was the basis of all of this, is that you have really worked to ensure that it is actually a matter of participation, including participation and decision, not just a participation that looks so that, as you now propose with this citizen's request, I can send a question – but not all alone, but must send it together with 9 999 other people, so that what Mr Stefan has already done here is done. So there is a high obstacle to being able to bring it in at all. You must not even come here to ask the question yourself, but it is read by one of us here. One does not even have the opportunity to ask again, just as we parliamentarians have it in a second round, but that also does the parliamentary parties again. And if the question does not come to the call, it will be answered within two months. We all, who are in opposition or who have already sat, know how requests from ministers are answered. This is a frustration programme, which you provide here for the people who take on many obstacles and then get similar answers as we do, namely: I can't answer that. On Question 2: No, no, no, The administrative burden does not allow me to answer. Or: Read in a request answer from Germany! And much more; we could now lead a whole latte. [[Congratulation with the greens.]] But we must always talk about this in detail, how we are dealing with Parliamentarians' interpollation rights here. So this citizen's request gets a clear no from us. It's a long time off. This is characterized by a paternalist-democratic basic attitude. myparliament.at, my fellow Members.at, that works much more easily, and people get answers there as well. Why should they take this and collect ten thousand signatures? But, of course, it is also a question of transparency that could be introduced here, and we made a very clear excuse last week. It is also timely to allow transparency to be exercised by itself in a democratic state and not to wait for one to be asked. That is to say, a real freedom of information law, which includes, on the one hand, easy to find in all the administrative pieces where it is possible; and the possibility of a simplified provision of information, without having to place the silence of office as a bulwark between oneself and the citizens. Yes, the discussions that we have led to direct democracy and, above all, to three-stage national legislation, which we also propose, of course include many points that can be seen skeptically and which must also be discussed: the question of the party landscape, the question of who can afford such popular desires, and, of course, the question of the tension in which such a national legislation stands with the elected Parliament and with a government to make decisions. But if you are sitting in the working group, Madam President Prammer, Mr Klubobmann Cap and Mr Klubobmann Kopf, and say that there are still so many questions that need to be discussed, then I must say to you: We have been sitting in this working group for almost a year, and these questions have already been identified at the beginning, which we could have discussed for a year, we had a hearing where many experts have expressed their opinion – and nothing has gone on because nothing should go on. So you can't argue here that we still need time. We can still make a commission of inquiry and use thousands of working groups, but these proposals will go as well as the administrative reform that has been slumbering in the boxes for decades, and sometime then it will be so far that there will have to be a pressure from outside so that something goes on here. There is also an exciting proposal from the initiatives. The democratic initiatives have proposed: Well, if the parliamentarians cannot jump over their own interests as party politicians, we make a citizens' assembly where a proposal is drawn up according to certain representative compositions. Parliament can then draw up a counter-proposal or several counter-proposals. And then we let the people vote on it. I think that is very welcome. It would be exciting what comes up with proposals because we don't really know how many people would be involved. The national survey on compulsory military service has shown: As soon as there is a prospect that the result will be declared binding – that was then also stated in the follow-up surveys – it is quite a motivator for people to go – and one must take that seriously. But one must also take seriously to think about how the questions come about. We have already discussed this today about the military debate. Here, too, one could think of a procedure that is not then agreed in individual party chambers, which is being questioned, but that we are really taking a democratic path of participation, which questions are presented. So what you have presented here as a democratic package is far away from a democratic package, as we imagine it. At the moment, this cannot be our agreement at all, but it is of course good democratic culture not to conclude negotiations. We now have a six-week review period, where I hope that many people, many initiatives, will participate. We'll have another expert hearing. Then it's about negotiating that, and then we'll see if you get the two-thirds in the house or not. But a reform, as we imagine it, is not.

ParlaMint-AT_2013-01-31-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00188_d7e25035	Madam President. Ladies and gentlemen! Yesterday we discussed the establishment of administrative courts, and that was a legislative process – and that was what we all praised here – as he should be, where one really felt that the government had an interest in a common solution with the Secretary of State Ostermayer, where the opposition – no matter where and with what approaches it came from – has brought in ideas that have also taken up, discussed and, as far as possible, have been implemented. And so there has really been a common package. That's how you should do it. Today we are discussing a package to reform the right to vote, direct democracy, the Rules of Procedure, wherever one might think, this is from us, this is an initiative of the Members here, as we are trying, regardless of whether we are the government party or the opposition party, to bind a package together. What do we hear today? Mr Cap has at least said that this is an initiative by the government parties. Mr Gerstl says that this is an initiative by Mr Kurz – and that is it now! Madam President, I somehow remembered this differently, and you were also somewhat surprised when a few days ago the end of the working group was announced and the government parties presented this package, according to the motto: That's it now! So I have seen my job in this working group and the task of the working group different from being a source of ideas for government parties, for a State Secretary, where nothing more happens for months, then the smallest common denominator is presented to us and we are told: That's it now! We shall now discuss this, and that will now be decided. [[BZÖ's condolences.]] If so, ladies and gentlemen, I'll tell you: Do it yourself in the future, you don't need us! If we are only to act as a giver of ideas, if we are serious hours and hours, we are really trying seriously to get rid of maximum demands and try to bind a common package, and then we are presented by the government, then I can only say to you: Do it yourself! And we do something different in our time. Let us then put this to us, and we will discuss this here, put us in the committee, and where we believe that it is appropriate, we agree, and where we do not think so, we reject it. But that's really a placebo, colleague Cap, and, I don't know, maybe a little bad conscience. I do not want to be too secret now, but I felt that the President – she was the president of this working group – really had the intention of finding a common solution and integrating the opposition, and herself was surprised at this result. If one goes back in history, it must not be forgotten that the legislative initiative, which leads to a successful popular desire, was a point of discussion, but also – Mr Cap, I think it was an initiative of yours – the reduction of the National Council. That's all going down now. This too was originally part of the discussion that we said: Today, in the 21st century. In the 19th century, one must start with administrative savings also at the top and question whether we really need 183 national councillors, whether the Federal Council in its composition is still necessary today, whether all the land days in the composition – 100 MPs in Vienna, 1,200 district mandates in Vienna, all paid mandates! – are still necessary and adequate today. That was at the heart of our discussions. [[Call to the ÖVP: What does the BZÖ do?]] - Yeah, what's the BZÖ doing?- Oh, my God! This is the only concern of the EIA MP. [[BZÖ's condolences.]] That's the difference to us: We are not concerned with the mandates. We're not about positions like you. We are concerned about the country, we are concerned about the future of Austria, dear colleague of the Austrian People's Party! [[BZÖ's condolences.]] That's panoptic, that's museum, your political museum! Wonderful, wonderful! I really thank you for such calls; perhaps a little bit of them, then we have something to discuss. That is the problem, however, and I do not think, Mr Cap, that is what you wanted. But with such concreteists going into the bodies, of course this is difficult. It would be nice to have a free discussion here, to actually live the free mandate, instead of hanging on the coalition chain, to say, when it comes to the Rules of Procedure, when it comes to voting rights, when it comes to the composition of the bodies, we are not listening to the votes of the Austrian People's Party and these concreteists, but we are really going in the direction of what is modern and future-oriented. And there, I think, we would have brought together more than this package. I do not want to criticize this now in terms of soil and soil, there are also many things that we have called for, for example, the online support of instruments of direct democracy, which is good and right. And we will also discuss very intensively here and try to get some positive. The fact that the value of the preferred votes is strengthened in the right to vote is also positive, no question at all. However, the great mission we have given ourselves in this working group was not fulfilled: that we bring a modern right to vote, also a modern way of dealing with the instruments of direct democracy. This also shows the last popular survey, even if it is so cheered up by some here: that without objective information from the population, these instruments can unfortunately also be used politically. This is an important point, and we have discussed this in this working group. But of these results there is nothing with it. So we're unfortunately stuck in the beginnings. I have a certain amount of rest hope that we can improve one or another in the Committee. But there's really no reason for great lobbying here. It's just a question of being able to say we brought something together again. We also see this in other areas: two years' standstill in many working groups and subcommittees. And now we are saying, hold, now we have elections, we have to put together some kind of balance sheet, and something is being presented quickly. This is a little too little, and we will not be available as a majority.
